84
Other 
competencies 
addressed
Safe use of materials and various tools, demonstrates coordination and 
flexibility, cooperative behaviour, helpfulness.
What can be 
observed?
Demonstrates awareness of safety, self-care, and builds on the related 
vocabulary.
C–1.5: Understands the importance of safety in movements 
(walking, running, cycling) and acts appropriately
Activity
Road Safety
Material required
Red, yellow and green colour cards (Big size)
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
Help children learn three main traffic colours: red, green, and yellow. 
Arrange two boards: green coloured and red coloured. Let the children 
stand in a circle. Give instructions: when they see a green board, they 
must run, and when they see a red one, they must stop. Enable them to 
relate their specific actions with red colour and green colour with respect 
to traffic lights. Discuss the concept of traffic lights with them.
4 – 5 years 
Make one child wear a traffic light outfit (made up of cardboard/cloth/
chart paper) and let other children say: ‘Red light, red light; what do 
you say?’. The child wearing the outfit says, ‘stop, stop, stop’. Then 
other children ask: ‘Green light, green light, what do you say?’ The child 
wearing the outfit says, ‘Go, go, go’. Similarly the yellow light says ‘wait, 
wait, wait’.
5 – 6 years 
Draw a big square on the ground and make a child stand with three 
colour cards in the square. At some distance draw a road with zebra 
crossing lines. Ask other children to act as they are driving on the road. 
Now let the child in the square show green colour. You call out the name 
of the colour loudly to reinforce. Give instruction to the children that 
they should drive on the road when they see/hear green. When they 
see/hear red, they have to stand still; when they see/hear yellow, they 
have to wait and be alert. With this activity, they will learn about road 
crossing and road safety rules. Talk to them why they should follow the 
traffic signals.
Variation
•	 Let the children make traffic lights using red, green and yellow 
coloured paper as an art and craft activity and talk about it. Recite 
the related rhymes/poems. 
•	 Show a video of traffic signal activity/songs on road safety/organise 
role play, etc.


Annamaya Kosha and Pranamaya Kosha
85
Other 
competencies 
addressed
Develop awareness for self-protection, practice safe use of materials and 
simple tools, shapes, colours, precision and control in working with their 
hands and fingers, etc.
What can be 
observed?
The children identify traffic lights and other traffic symbols also such as 
zebra crossing, follow road safety rules and avoid danger.
C–1.6: Understands unsafe situations and asks for help
Activity
Storytelling/Puppet Show
Material required
Picture cards, story cards, puppets
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
•	 Narrate a story to give a message on not to accept toys, chocolates, 
money or other things from strangers.
•	 Narrate a story to make them aware to maintain safe distance from 
ponds, lakes, rivers, etc.
Variation
•	 Talk about the safety of body parts. Ask the children about safe and 
unsafe situations by showing the given situations on chart papers: 
playing on road, going near fire, putting finger in eyes/nose, keeping 
hand/face outside bus window, walking on footpath, walk on left side 
of the road, etc. Let them discuss which situation is safe and which 
one is unsafe. 
•	 Also, talk to them about the risks involved in going near burning stove/
gas burners, switches/cables, walking barefoot, etc.

have 
open 
communication with their parents 
or teachers about their safety. 
Reassure them that their safety is of 
our concern and whenever they feel 
unsafe, they should go and talk to 
a trusted family member or teacher.
Variation
•	 Prepare worksheet/picture cards related to Dos and Don’ts related to 
safety concerns. 
•	 Help them memorise the phone numbers of parents and know their 
home address.
•	 Show small cartoon films/stories concerning safe and unsafe situations. 
5 – 6 years 
•	 Narrate stories or organise a drama/role play to make children aware 
about their safety and approach a trusted adult (parent/teacher/
guardian), if they are uncomfortable from anyone in the family or 
outside.
•	 Tell them not to go anywhere without informing the teacher or parents/
family members. Always use main road to go to the school/playground 
and 
avoid 
shortcuts 
and 
isolated areas.
•	 Tell them to say ‘No’, if 
someone tries to touch them 
or make them feel scared, 
uncomfortable or confused. 
(Although we should avoid 
making children fearful of 
unfamiliar people, we want 
them to be cautious and 
not overly trusting even if a 
stranger is friendly or looks 
nice)
Variation
•	 Organise storytelling sessions using puppets on safe and unsafe 
situations.
•	 Matching activity—Let the children match the picture of community 
helpers like doctor, fire-fighter, police with their roles. Discuss how 
they make us feel safe and protected.
Other 
competencies 
addressed
The child understands safe and unsafe situations; develops qualities like 
self-reliance, leadership, positive learning habits; follows instructions; 
develops memory, attention, problem-solving skills and self-protection.
What can be 
observed?
The child recognises known and unknown persons/things; communicates 
discomfort, maintains distance from strangers, seeks help, etc.

C–2.1: Differentiates between shapes, colours and their shades
Activity 1
Identifying Colours
Material required
Different colours, charts, cards, fruits, vegetables, etc., origami 
paper
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
(Matching colours)
Use different coloured objects (primary colours only) in the class such 
as toys, blocks, charts, pictures of flowers, fruits, vegetables, etc. 
•	 Talk about colours 
using these support 
materials and let 
children identify 
colours and name 
them.
•	 Let children match the 
colour of each other’s 
dresses.
•	 Organise activities 
with coloured beads; 
threading beads in a 
colour sequence.
CG – 2: Children Develop Sharpness in Sensorial 
Perceptions
•	 Sensory development opportunities for taste, smell, sight, sound and touch
•	 Activities related to tasting and guessing, exploring taste with edible fruits/
vegetables and naming them
•	 Fun with bottles having different smells
•	 Creating shapes with scented play dough
•	 Experimenting with objects that smells
•	 Name/letter hunt
•	 I spy games, playing hide and seek, blindfold games
•	 Putting sound boxes in sequence from loudest to softest and vice versa
•	 Outdoor sound hunt
•	 Sorting textured letters


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
88
•	 Organise simple origami activities with children like making 
cap, boat, etc., using different coloured paper.
•	 Take a particular object like red coloured toy and let children 
find the same colour in the class or around themselves. 
•	 Sing rhymes and songs on colours.
•	 Prepare three placards of different colours like red, yellow and 
green and place them in the three corners of the class. You stand 
the fourth corner with the children. Play dhapli and then stop 
and say the name of one colour, like green. Let all children run 
towards the corner having green colour. Similarly, give chance 
to every child to continue the game with different colours.
4 – 5 years 
(Sorting)
•	 Discuss different colours and get the children to sort and 
classify objects based on colours. Once children reach the stage 
of naming colours, give out colour cards to them. Play a simple 
game of classification.
•	 For example call out, ‘Red’. All the children with red cards will 
come forward. Similarly, call out 'Green". Only the 'greens' will 
come forward, and so on.
Variation
Prepare several different colour cards (red, blue, yellow) and give 
one card to each child. Place one colour card in the centre. Say the 
name of the card placed in the center (like red) and let them look at 
their card, identify the colour and match their card with the card 
placed in the centre. Children having red colour card will come 
forward and place their card in the centre (matching-matching).
5 – 6 years
•	 Discuss colours in nature like colour of leaf, flowers, etc., 
during circle time. Ask the children to draw any scenery like 
garden, farm, market, etc. Drawing and colouring provides lots 
of opportunities to experiment with colours.
•	 Let the children experiment with colour-mixing to make new 
shades.
•	 Make colour cards of five different shades. Let them arrange 
these cards from light to dark.
•	 Prepare colour dominoes cards/blocks and let children match 
colours. Let them solve simple riddles related to colours.
•	 Make three large circles. Place a red object in one, a blue object 
in the second and a yellow object in the third. Give red, blue 
and yellow coloured cards to all the children in the class. Beat a 
drum and let the children run around. As soon as the drum-beat 
stops, the children should run towards the circle that has the 
object whose colour matches the colour of card in their hand.
•	 Take the children on a nature-walk to observe colours around 
them.


Annamaya Kosha and Pranamaya Kosha
89
Variation
•	 Find objects of the same colour in the classroom.
•	 Let the children match each other’s clothes on the basis of 
colour.
•	 Play circle game with the children holding colour cards for 
matching.
•	 Teach rhymes and songs on colour concept.
•	 Play games of classification of colours.
•	 Play a game— “tippi-tippi tap what colour you want”.
•	 Let the children solve simple riddles on colours.
•	 Help children learn and speak out the names of colours.
•	 Organise drawing and painting activities as they give children a 
lot of opportunities to experiment with colours.
•	 Threading of coloured beads in a pattern or sequence, for 
example, red, blue, green, and again red, blue, green and so on 
is another interesting activity.
For Inclusivity: Combine the concepts of texture and colours and 
let the child sort textured and coloured cards. 
For Gifted Children: Colour mixing using different colours using 
gelatin papers of one colour, place second colour paper behind it, 
a third colour appears.
(Such activities need to be planned for different diversities among 
children. Two examples have been presented here).

addressed
Creative and aesthetic skills, language skills, observes and 
understands shapes, sorts objects into groups, uses words, 
understand and gives oral instructions, sensorial perception, 
shows coordination and movments, appreciates, nature.
What can be observed?
Children’s interest; participation level in the activity; vocabulary; 
differentiates and names colours, groups objects on the basis of 
colour, shades of a colour, predicts resulting colour, experiments 
with and uses colour in drawings, etc.

Activity 2
Identifying Shapes (Indoor Play)
Material required
Shape puzzles, wooden or plastic blocks, paper, clay, chalk, bottle 
caps, pebbles, blocks, shape cards, dominoes, thread, card board, 
different shape cards, puzzles, crayons and sand
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years
•	 Through guided conversation children can be made aware of 
the basic shapes in our environment. Give them shape puzzles 
to fix, and talk about shapes.
•	 Let them colour different shapes during art activities.
Variation
•	 Sing rhymes and songs related to shapes, like chanda gol, suraj 
gol, etc. 
•	 Ask simple riddles on shapes.
4 – 5 years
In creative activities, like tearing and pasting, and clay work, 
children can be given different shapes and asked to cut into small 
pieces and paste them on a design, or on clay models of different 
shapes. By the time children are four years of age, they can draw 
circles free-handed and name all three basic shapes like circle, 
square and triangle.
Variation
•	 Make a circle, a square and a triangle on the ground with a 
chalk or with a stick, on wet ground. 
•	 Provide bottle caps or pebbles and let the children place them 
on the outline of the shapes given.
•	 Play circle games, let them make big circle, small circle, etc.
5 – 6 years
For construction activities, use blocks or any material of different 
shapes, such as cartons, tins, match boxes, etc. Let children 
identify the shapes and use them appropriately in the construction 
activities. Discuss different shapes with them, and allow them to 
talk about shapes.
Variation
•	 Rhymes and songs related to shapes help in associating the 
shapes with different objects and remembering the names of 
the shapes. 
•	 Let the children place shape cards (embossed/textured/tactile 
on one another such as circle on circle, square on square, 
triangle on triangle, etc.


Annamaya Kosha and Pranamaya Kosha
91
•	 Take different colour papers from old newspapers/magazines 
and origami paper and cut shapes like circle, triangle and 
square from each colour paper. Mix them up. Let them select 
the common shapes having different colours, odd-one-out strips 
can also be made.
•	 Shape dominoes cards can be made with two shapes on each 
domino card. Ask children to make a pattern with the dominoes 
by placing them in such a way that two adjacent shapes are 
alike.
•	 Make the outlines of a circle, a square and a triangle on wooden 
boards with the help of nails.  Cover the top of the nails so that 
it will not harm the children. Let the children wind a thread 
along these nails and create shapes. Let them complete the 
pattern or extend the pattern related to shapes.
•	 Let the children explore how to fold a paper to make paper 
shapes (boat, box, hat, basket, house, animal, etc.); show them 
how to cut paper into strips using child-friendly scissors and 
how to make a collage from paper scraps.
•	 Make shape-puzzles with cardboard cut-outs of different 
shapes. Let them put together and create different formations, 
for example, figure of a man, a bird, a house, etc.
Other competencies 
addressed
Able to match the same shapes, develops physical skills, social skills, 
concept formation, demonstrates  coordination and willingness 
and participation, uses appropriate tools, sorts objects, identifies 
patterns, understands and gives oral instructions, explores and 
plays, works collaboratively.
What can be observed?
The child identifies shapes, makes patterns, solves puzzles, 
groups various shapes, colours and shades, uses appropriate 
shaped objects during activities. Groups on the basis of 
visual characteristics like all yellow circles together, all leaves 
together, etc.


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
92
Activity 3
Game of Shapes (Outdoor Play)
Material required
Blocks, picture and alphabet cards, chalk, music instruments
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years
Play a game of shapes. Draw a large circle, a square and a triangle 
on the ground with chalk (or with a stick if the ground is wet). Play 
the drum or dafli and let the children run around. Stop playing 
the drum/dafli and call out the name of a shape, for example, say 
‘circle’. All the children should try and get into the circle. Those 
who go to the wrong shape or are left outside will have to wait.
Variation
•	 Let the children run here and there. Play dafli and call them. 
Ask them to form a circle. Guide them to walk in a circle on the 
beats of dafli like fast, slow and very slow.
•	 You can draw big square or triangle and ask children to walk on 
its outline fast, slow and very slow.
4 – 5 years
Play a game of shapes with the children. Let them sit in a circle. 
The teacher starts the game by saying ‘Be quick’, tell me the 
name of things that are square, etc. It must be said in rhythm 
accompanied by clapping of hands and snapping of fingers. In the 
same rhythm, the child answers the name of an object which is 
of that particular shape (e.g., slate). Same can be played for other 
shapes like circle, triangle and square.
5 – 6 years
•	 Get the children to build structures of different shapes using 
sand, blocks, etc., outdoors.
•	 Give them clay to make different shapes.
•	 Let them draw any figure using circle, triangle and square only.
Variation
Oraganise nature walks and field trips where children can 
recognise and identify various shapes.
Other competencies 
addressed
Physical and motor skills; the child shows balance, flexibility in 
activities; shows cooperation, differentiates shapes, social skills, 
positive learning habits, coordination.
What can be observed?
Observes accuracy in recognition of shapes, cooperation while 
playing, enjoyment, goal-directed actions and cleaning up.

C–2.2: Develops visual memory for symbols and representations
Activity
Know Symbols and Spot the Differences
Material required
Blocks, beads, cards, self-corrective picture cards
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years
•	 In the block building area, place blocks of different shapes and 
make the children sit around them. Then ask the children to 
match blocks of the same colour or shape. Give chance to each 
and every child.
•	 Ask the children to sort beads of same colour or shape.
•	 Show three basic shape cards and place them one after the 
other on the table. Ask them to close their eyes and remove one 
shape card. Let them open their eyes and find what’s missing?
Variation
•	 Prepare name cards for all the children. Encourage them to find 
their own card (Use this for attendance activity. Those children 
who are present can be asked to put their name cards in a 
designated place. Teacher can easily identify who is absent)
4 – 5 years
•	 Let the children do the activity, ‘Odd one out’. Prepare strips 
with three to four pictures on it with one different and three 
similar. Let them spot the one that is different, e.g., three 
vegetables and one fruit.
•	 Prepare strips with three different pictures on it. Keep the cut-
outs of those three pictures separately. Then give one picture 
out of the three to a child and ask him/her to find the same on 
the strip and to place the card on the matching picture. Let all 
other children clap for the child.
5 – 6 years
•	 Expose the children to the letters of the L1/familiar language, 
when doing activities with beginning and end sounds. They will 
learn to recognise a letter by associating the sound with the 
symbol.


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
94
•	 Make self-corrective (embossed/tactile) picture cards (of 
pictures and its name) in the form of puzzles. Jumble them up. 
Ask each child to match the pictures with their sound.
•	 Call out words and let children find the related pictures.
•	 Give 
pictures 
of 
familiar objects like 
‘tub’, 
‘tree’, 
‘cup’, 
‘balloon’, etc. Make 
the beginning sound 
of any object like 
‘t’ and let children 
match the sound and 
look for the picture of 
that sound.
•	 Make strips having 
pictures of objects. Each strip can have four pictures. Of these, 
the names of three should have the same beginning sound. The 
fourth  should be different. Ask children to identify the picture 
with  different sound.
•	 Make strips as explained for 'Odd one out'. Instead of pictures 
or designs use letters of the alphabet and words for 5-6 age 
group. Let the children identify different letters and words.
•	 Make any sound like ‘ब’ let children match the sound with the 
letter of the alphabet, ‘B’ and make sound-letter  correspondence 
with the support of picture.
Variation
•	 Make two identical pictures with only one prominent difference 
between them. Let the children spot the difference.
•	 In the reading area, provide picture cards, picture books and let 
children flip through the pages in the right direction.
•	 Give story cards or alphabet cards and let children arrange 
sequentially from left to right direction.
•	 Worksheets can be given to children to encourage working from 
left to right direction.
Other competencies 
addressed
Differentiates sounds, willingness to participate, demonstrates 
appropriate vocabulary, understands oral instructions, recognises 
and says, develops phonological awareness, explores and plays 
with variety of materials.
What can be observed?
Recognition of shapes and naming them, matches symbols with 
same pattern, memory, recalling, cooperation while playing, 
enjoyment, goal-directed actions and cleaning up.

C–2.3: Differentiates sounds and sound 
patterns by their pitch, volume and tempo
Activity 1
Identify the Sound
Material required
Music instruments, audio devices for different sounds
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years
•	 Ask children to imitate sounds and movements of different 
animals like those of a dog, cow, cat, bird, etc.
•	 Let them identify the sounds of different vehicles like motorcycle, 
bus, car, auto, train.
•	 Let them listen to the sounds in the environment carefully.
•	 Let the children close their eyes, listen to the sound, identify the 
sound and tell about the sound they have heard, e.g., sound 
of fan, closing and opening the door, bird chirping, clapping 
sound and so on.
•	 Play audio of animal and bird sounds and ask the children to 
recognise them. 
•	 Play musical instruments having different sounds. Ask children 
to close their eyes, listen to it and identify the instruments.
4 – 5 years
Let the children listen to popular rhymes and identify sounds, 
pitch (high, low) and rhythm.
Variation
•	 Allow children to play simple musical instruments to understand 
sounds.
5 – 6 years
•	 Let them identify the sound and tell which sound they have 
heard, e.g., birds’ and animals’ sounds, human voices, children 
playing, etc. 
•	 Ask children to listen to the first sound they hear in the morning 
and share.
•	 Take the children out on a field trip to a nearby playground/
garden/sanctuary/zoo where they get chance to see and hear 
the sounds of animals and birds.
•	 Get children to sing songs at different pitches.
•	 Expose children to various Indian musical instruments, dholak, 
harmonium, flute, dafli, drum, etc. Let the children try playing 
them. Get the children to discuss sounds such as pitch (Uucha 
Swar, Neecha Swar), tone, etc.
Other competencies 
addressed
Sense of sound, memory, environmental awareness, enjoyment, 
appreciation of sounds and music.
What can be observed?
Ability to recognise and differentiate between sounds, ability to 
produce sounds, enjoyment, etc.


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
96
Activity 2
Let us Dance to the Beats
Material required
Vacant space, music system, suitable songs and musical 
instruments
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years
•	 Play a cheerful beat song and allow the children to dance to its 
beats; let them recognise fast beats and slow beats and enjoy!
4 – 5 years
•	 Play a song with fast beats and then a song with slow beats. 
Let children experience the differences in dancing to the two 
different beats.
•	 Teach simple classical dance movements to all children with 
fingers and eye movements.
5 – 6 years
•	 Get children to note even the pitch and tone of the song, along 
with beats, and show expressions while dancing.
•	 Allow children to play musical instruments like dafli, dholak, 
harmonium, etc.
•	 Jaltarang activity to let the children identify different sounds 
from different levels of water in a cup.
Variation
National festivals are celebrated in all schools. Children of all age 
groups should be encouraged to participate in these celeberations 
through dance and music. Prepare a dance involving all the 
children for participating in the celebration of national festivals.
Other competencies 
addressed
Music, physical skills, and creativity in body movements, 
enjoyment, works collaboratively in the arts, explores and plays 
with own voice, creates dance and movements, appreciates and 
experiences different forms of art.
What can be observed?
Active participation, creative body movements, enjoyment, facial 
expressions, eye movement, finger movement, etc.

Activity 3
Listen and Tell 
Material required
Different objects which make sound, audio system as simple as a 
cell phone or audio system can be used
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 6 years
•	 Let children identify sounds of different objects like whistle, 
drum, keys, etc.
•	 Ask the children to close their eyes. Make sounds of dog, cat, 
crow and ask them to identify the sounds.
•	 Let all children in the class close their eyes. Make a sound of 
bangle, dafli, ghungroo, etc. and ask children to identify the 
sound.
•	 Sing a song ‘मैैं तो सो रही थी, मुझे बिल््ली ने जगाया, बोलली म्याऊँ म्याऊँ म्याऊँ...’
•	 Let the children produce the sounds of known animals 
(domestic/wild).
•	 Ask the children to wear costumes/masks of animals and birds 
and do a role-play.
Variation
Let one children speak and others guess who is speaking. 
Other competencies 
addressed
Distinguishes between slow and fast tempo. Recognition of animal-
sound, creates sounds, understands oral instructions, appreciates 
simple songs, helps each other.
What can be observed?
Active participation, accuracy of identification, socialisation, 
differentiates between sounds in the environment, humans, 
animals, vehicles, loud and soft sound, etc.
C–2.4: Differentiates multiple smells and tastes
Activity 1
Smell and Tell
Material required
Some fruits, cooked food, agarbatti, dettol, perfume, etc.
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years
•	 Let the children sit in a circle and discuss different fruits; 
familiarise them with smell of banana, orange, guava, etc.
•	 A play activity: Let the children close their eyes make them 
smell a rose and then hide it. Ask them to identify the smell.


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
98
Variation
•	 Similarly let them identify the smells of agarbatti, dettol, 
perfume, etc.
•	 Make simple chart with two halves labelled ‘Good Smell’ and 
‘Bad Smell’.
4 – 5 years
•	 Let children close their eyes and identify things by smelling, for 
example coffee, orange, mint, etc.
•	 Before serving snacks or meal, let the children close their eyes 
and use their sense of smell to guess what is being served like 
pickle, kheer, etc. 
•	 Bring strong smelling familiar objects, for example, agarbatti, 
perfume, wet soil, etc., into the class. Let each child close his/
her eyes and try to identify the object from its smell. Include 
some objects which have no smell, for example, sugar, water, etc.
•	 During art and craft activities, let the children identify scented 
markers/smelly play dough/scented eraser/glue, etc.
•	 Take the children on a nature walk and ask them to observe 
and point out the objects with pleasant and unpleasant smells.
Variation
•	 Take the children on a walk into a herbal garden and let them 
observe and smell various leaves with different smell and talk 
about them, like tulsi, karipatta, ajwain, dhania, pudina, etc.
5 – 6 years
•	 Give the children a cooked dish to taste, like halwa and let them 
recognise the smell and taste. Let them express their happiness 
through emojis.
•	 Collect small plastic bottles, 
cotton balls and items for 
smelling like perfume, heeng, 
coffee, etc., put cotton ball into 
three different bottles. Place 
few drops of coffee solution, 
heeng paste, perfume on each 
cotton ball and make ‘smelling 
or scented bottles’. Dip the 
cotton balls in the coffee 
solution, heeng, perfume, and 
put the cotton ball in bottles 
and close the lid. Play ‘smell and identify’ game.
•	 Make picture cards showing different objects like garlic, rose, 
lime, different fruits, talcum powder, manure, as well as objects/
situations like overflowing garbage cans, etc. Let them classify 
these on the basis of pleasant and unpleasant smells.
•	 Talk about the nose that helps us to smell, like in humans, along 
with how various animals and insects smell. Develop a vocabulary 
related to smell like flowery, sweet, stinky, smoky, etc.


Annamaya Kosha and Pranamaya Kosha
99
Variation
•	 Collect wrappers of different soaps like neem, tulsi, rose, 
jasmine, glycerin, medicated soap, etc., and let the children 
identify the type of the soap.
•	 Call out the name of a food item and ask them to name its smell 
(pleasant, unpleasant) or taste (sweet, salty).
•	 Let the children mime their reactions to various smells in a 
game situation. Ask them to sit in a semi-circle. Call out the 
name of any object with a typical smell. Ask the children to 
respond with ‘Aha!” and appropriate expressions if the object

and related expressions.

Activity 2
Sense of Taste
Material required
Snack–time foods that children bring, sample foods of different 
tastes
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years
During snack-time, encourage children to sit in a circle, and eat 
together and recognise the taste of what they are eating, and tell 
each other.
Use snack-time to discuss the taste of various eatables.
You could show the food first and then ask them to guess the 
taste, like sweet or salty.
Variation
Cook together—Make something like kheer and let children smell 
and taste. Let them eat and enjoy with each other.
4 – 5 years
Bring samples of food items with distinct tastes for children, for 
example, lime, imli, sugar, salt, neem, etc. Let each child try and 
identify the different tastes and associate them with the food item.


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
100
Variation
Bring samples of food items. Ask the children to close their eyes 
and let them identify the tastes of different food items.
You can do this by relating to a theme such as fruits, vegetables 
and snacks and develop vocabulary like sour, bitter, sweet, etc.
5 – 6 years
•	 Let the children identify familiar foodstuff by tasting them. For 
example, ask the children to close their eyes and put a little 
piece of roti/potato/banana or any other commonly eaten food 
stuff into their mouth. Let the child guess what it is. Ask them 
about the taste.
•	 Talk about the colour, smell, texture, sound if any, like crunchy, 
soggy, etc.
•	 Let the children taste raw amla, cooked amla, dried and salted 
amla, sugar coated amla and find out their observations.
•	 Discuss about things that are not meant to be eaten, taste bad 
or could even make us sick.
Variation
Make some picture cards of food items with distinct tastes. Let 
the children classify these as salty, sour, sweet, bitter, and spicy. 
Show the children salt and powdered sugar side by side. Talk 
about how they look, smell and feel. Let the children taste and 
find which is salt and which one is sugar. Talk about things which 
are sweet and salty. 
Other competencies 
addressed
Language skills, socialisation,  shows liking for and understanding 
of nutritious food and does not waste food, self care and hygiene, 
differentiates smell and taste, like/dislike, perferences.
What can be observed?
Accuracy in identifying tastes, their enjoyment, knows different 
tastes, identifies—sweet, salty, bitter, spicy, etc.
C–2.5: Sense of Touch
Activity:
Sense of Touch
Material required
Touch cards, different textured natural materials, stationery items 
like papers, pencils, cloth bags for touch activities
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years
•	 Give children the opportunity to handle and freely manipulate 
different objects in the environment. Encourage them to 
compare objects by feeling their surfaces, and to use the words 
‘hard’ and ‘soft’ or ‘rough’ and ‘smooth’ appropriately.
•	 Give children an assortment of easily available objects, for 
example, leaves, twigs, cotton wool, tree bark, wood, mud, sand, 
pebbles, etc., and let them handle and sort out these in terms of 
hard and soft or rough and smooth.


Annamaya Kosha and Pranamaya Kosha
101
•	 Make touch cards with different kinds of cloth pieces, for 
example, satin, jute, cotton, wool, etc. Let children match cards 
according to texture.
4 – 5 years
•	 Make a ‘feely’ bag in which objects of different shapes and 
textures, for example, small wooden cubes, ball of wool/cotton, 
pencil, piece of cloth, stone, feather, 
etc., are filled. Let the children 
touch and feel the objects. Matching 
set of the same material can be 
kept outside the bag. Let each child 
feel any one of the objects outside 
and then try to trace the matching 
object in the bag only through 
touch, without looking into the bag.
•	 Make ‘touch cards’ using sandpaper of graded texture, kite 
paper, crepe paper, woollen material, satin, cotton cloth, jute, 
etc. Give any three touch cards to a child and ask him/her to 
arrange these from rough to smooth. The children can also be 
asked to classify these touch cards on the basis of texture—the 
rough ones in one pile and the smooth ones in another.
5 – 6 years
•	 Give the children more than three touch cards or objects of 
different textures at a time and ask them to arrange these in 
order from rough to smooth.
•	 Let children sit in a circle, 
one child can be asked to 
place the known objects or 
familiar objects like pencil, 
eraser, duster, etc. behind 
some of the children. Each 
child has to find out without 
looking back if there is 
something behind him/her. If there is something he/she has to 
identify and name the object merely by feeling it.
Other competencies 
addressed
Language skills, socialisation, sensorial perception, understands 
oral instructions, converses fluently, explores and plays with 
variety of objects, works collaboratively, recognises different 
emotions, shows cooperative behaviour.
What can be observed?
Accuracy in naming the ‘touch and feel’ of objects; ability to sort 
them accurately; differentiates hard and soft, hot and cold, rough 
and smooth surfaces; compares two objects based on hard and 
soft, hot and cold, rough and smooth with the right vocabulary.


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
102
C–2.6: Begins integrating sensorial perceptions to get a holistic 
awareness of their experiences
Activity
Making of Fruit Salad 
Material required
Fruits, lemon, salt, spoon, knife, plates
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
(For all age groups) 
•	 Allow children to observe the teacher making fruit salad. Get 
4–5 years and 5–6year-old children to assist in making the 
salad— washing the fruits, peeling them, removing seeds, 
handling, garnishing items, etc. While cutting, mixing and 
garnishing fruits, talk to children about their colours, textures, 
taste, smell, etc. 
•	 Let them eat the fruit salad and experience the taste  of different 
fruits in a fruit salad. 
•	 Help them use the vocabulary related to taste like sweet, sour, 
salty, etc.
•	 Let them know how the shape, colour, appearance, smell and 
taste of fruits is related to their names.
Variation
Encourage children to make sprouts salad/green salad following 
all the above steps. Use lunch time to discuss different smells, 
tastes and textures of foods they are eating.
Other competencies 
addressed
Cooperation, sharing, shows precision and control in working with 
their hands and fingers, shows liking for nutritious food; practices 
safe use of material and simple tools, differentiates tastes, 
understands and gives oral instructions, works collaboratively 
and creates.
What can be observed?
Accuracy of recognising different smells, textures and tastes, and 
having a whole some sensorial experience


GOAL
3
C–3.1 Shows coordination between sensorial perceptions and body 
movements in various activities
Activity 1
Throw and Catch the Ball
Material required
Small and big sized balls, balloons
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
•	 Give the children a ball (light weight) for free play.
•	 Let the children roll or throw the ball and let the others catch 
the ball. 
Variation
Blowing a balloon: Distribute balloons to each child. Ask the 
children to blow the balloon. Let the children play with balloon. 
Write their names on balloons.
4 – 5 years 
•	 Free play with ball/balloon.
•	 Let the children stand in a circle. Put a bucket or empty carton 
in the centre and ask each child to throw a ball into it.
•	 Let children stand in a circle. One child will hold the ball. 
Another child will take the name of any child standing in 
the circle. The child who has the ball, throws the ball to that 
particular child (the children  must be attentive in order to 
catch the ball).
Variation
•	 Let children make a pyramid of empty paper glasses and ask 
children to throw a ball at the pyramid. The pyramid falls 
down  on throwing the ball, ask children to build the pyramid
together again.
•	 Hang a tyre low from a tree or a beam and ask children to 
throw a ball through it.
CG – 3: Children develop fit and flexible body
•	 Development of gross motor skills (walking, running, jumping, hopping, crawling, 
climbing, rhythmic movement, throwing, catching, kicking, etc.)
•	 Fine motor skills and eye-hand coordination (threading, tearing, pasting, scrib­
bling, drawing, colouring, printing, moulding, paper folding, etc.)


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
104
5 – 6 years
•	 Activities for 3 – 4 years and 4 – 5 years are also suitable for 
5 – 6 years old children. 
•	 Ask the children to stand in a circle. You stand in the centre 
and throw the ball towards each child randomly, asking the 
child to catch it. The child who fails to catch the ball will come 
in the centre and throw the ball for others to catch, the game 
continues.
Variation
Let the children throw the light weight ball up and clap their 
hands and try to catch the ball.
Other competencies 
addressed
Concentration, attentiveness, cooperation, waiting for one’s turn.
What can be observed?
Estimation of the distance, physical fitness, flexible body, 
coordination, precision and control, strength and endurance. 
Activity 2
Kick a Ball
Material required
Ball, box, chalk
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years
Let the children stand in a row. Let each child attempt to kick the 
ball hard without specifying any direction.
Variation
Specify the direction and ask children to kick the ball.
4 – 5 years 
Place a box/stool at a short distance. Encourage each child to 
kick the ball towards it.
Variation
Keep an empty big carton box open from both the ends. Place it on 
the ground, let the children kick the ball through it.
5 – 6 years
Ask each child to kick the ball in the goalpost from a distance 
turn by turn.
Variation
Let the children stand in circle and kick the ball towards each 
other randomly, taking each name. Also they will try to stop the 
ball from going out of the circle.
Other competencies 
addressed
Concentration, coordination, body strength, estimation.
What can be observed?
The child is kicking the object and estimating the distance and 
speed, the child is concentrating on the target/goal.


Annamaya Kosha and Pranamaya Kosha
105
Activity 3
Rhythmic Movement
Material required
Dafli, dholak
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
Include music and rhythmic dance movements involving bending, 
swaying, jumping, walking by children either outdoors or indoors.
Variation
Use a dafli to provide the beat. Children can clap their hands 
according to the beat. Change the tempo of the beat from fast to 
slow and slow to fast.
4 – 5 years 
Ask the children to stand in a semi-circle. Sing a song or rhyme 
on body parts. The children will have to touch their body part(s) 
accordingly. 
Song: Head, shoulders, knees and toes.
Variation
Arrange a dafli or a dholak. Sing while playing the musical 
instruments and ask the children to dance to the tunes.
5 – 6 years
Musical chair: Place 5–6 chairs in a large circle. In case there are 
no chairs available, you can instead place papers, clothes, old tires, 
and mats. Keep one chair less than the number of children taking 
part in the game. Ask the children to run around the chairs and you 
being the organiser, beat a dafli/ play some music. Tell every child 
to find and occupy a chair as soon as you stop playing the dafli/
music. The child who does not get a chair at this point will play 
dafli. Now remove one of the chairs and continue the game until 
only one child is left. The last to get the chair  will be the winner.
Variation
Play any music patriotic song, festival song, etc., let the children 
dance according to the beats.
Other competencies 
addressed
Concentration, creative self-expression, phonological awareness-
rhyming, environmental concepts, vocabulary development.
What can be observed?
Shows rhythmic body movements on the beat and willingness to 
participate actively (sing along and dance).


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
106
Activity 4
Creeping (Crawling) Rolling
Material required
Dafli, Rope
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
Beat the dafli and say any local animal name, e.g., ‘monkey’ or 
‘rabbit’ and let the children move like them on the beat.
Variation
Ask children to roll like a ball.
4 – 5 years 
Let two children hold a rope at a low height and get the other 
children to crawl under it one by one and run back.
Variation
Similarly you can ask the children to cross over the rope held at 
low height or walk on the rope spread on the floor in a zig-zag 
manner.
5 – 6 years
Ask the children to roll forward and backward and do rock and 
roll on the ground .
Variation
Let the children sing “let’s go rolling, rolling, rolling”, while making 
actions with hands.
Other competencies 
addressed
Neuromuscular coordination, body movement, concentration, 
decision-making, socio-emotional development.
What can be observed?
The child shows flexibility and body movement, follows the correct 
instructions and completes the activity, shows active participation, 
understands safe and unsafe situations, shows awareness of 
safety in movements.


Annamaya Kosha and Pranamaya Kosha
107
C– 3.2 Shows balance, coordination, and flexibility
 in various physical activities
Activity
Let us Balance Our Body
Material required
Plank, rope, hoop
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
Place a long and narrow wooden plank on the bricks for support.
Get all the children to stand in a row and walk one by one on the 
plank, without stepping sideways or using any support. Provide 
support when needed.
Variation
If a plank is not available, a straight rope could be placed on 
the floor. The plank or the rope should be short and straight. 
Gradually its length can be increased. Rope can be placed in a 
zig-zag manner on the floor on which children can walk.
4 – 5 years 
Draw two parallel lines on the floor. Let the children walk between 
the given lines.
Variation
•	 Let the children play on slopes where they get a chance to walk 
up the inclined position. 
•	 Ask them to hop forward on one foot, once or multiple times.
5 – 6 years
•	 Organise hurdle race. Place wooden planks, tyres one after the 
other on which the children have to walk/jump. Two to three short 
planks/old tyres could be used. Place these in a triangular shape 
or in a zig-zag shape to make the balancing activity more complex.
•	 Organise a race for the children in which they have to walk 
carrying some objects on their heads or in their hands, like a 
book on their head, or a glass of water in their hands (without 
spilling the water).
Variation
(All activities given for 
3 – 4 years or 4 – 5 years 
can also be done by 5 – 6 
years age group.) 
•	 Let the children dramatise the situation like making a bridge 
with wooden planks and let them walk on it as if they are 
walking on the bridge and there is water on all sides. You may 
say ‘walk carefully, do not fall into the water’.
•	 Ask the children to balance and spin the hoop around their 
waist 5 to 10 times and gradually increase the number of spins 
as long as they can. Encourage them to try spinning hoops on 
other body parts (arm or leg).
Other competencies 
addressed
Body strength, focus and concentration, flexibility, self-care, safe 
use of material, safety in movements, balance, coordination and 
flexibility in various physical activities.
What can be observed?
The child follows the instructions, balances the body and develops 
body awareness, interacts comfortably, shows cooperative 
behaviour, responds positively. 


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
108
C–3.3  Shows precision and control in working
with their hands and fingers
Activity 1
Threading the Beads/Flowers/Leaves
Material required
Thread, beads, bowl, pencil, colour
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
Give the children some beads in a bowl. These beads should 
be preferably of different colours. Let the children put these 
into thread or wire. (To make it a little more challenging, string 
beads with a specific sequence of colours and ask the children to 
complete the string in the same sequence, for example, red, blue, 
yellow, repeated in the same order). Flowers/leaves can be used 
for making a string.
4 – 5 years 
Provide cardboard and ask the children to cut it into their choice of 
shape or size. They may use crayons, colour pencils, water colours, 
thread, etc., to colour them. A small hole can be made to hang, let 
children use thread and make it a wall art. You may hang these 
wall arts in the classroom to motivate and encourage the students.
Variation
Make a ‘threading frame’ with a piece of cardboard (old copy cover). 
Draw an outline of any simple object, for example, tree or fish and 
punch it with holes all along the outline. Let the children lace these 
outlines with a stiff thread, wire or even with an old shoelace.
5 – 6 years
Provide the children with outlines of complex shapes of animals, 
letters of alphabets, etc., punched (small holes) all along the 
outline. Let the children lace these outlines with a stiff wire or 
a thread. These outlines can be made on old copy covers, old 
greeting cards, etc.
Other competencies 
addressed
Eye-hand coordination, concentration, visual motor skills, positive 
learning habits, works collaboratively, innovates, problem solving, 
sharpness in sensorial perception.
What can be observed?
The child puts the beads or shoelace into the desired space and 
develops hand movement of gripping objects, creativity, eye-hand 
coordination.


Annamaya Kosha and Pranamaya Kosha
109
Activity 2
Let us Enjoy Tearing-Cutting-Pasting 
Material required
Paper, glue
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
Provide the children with some waste paper, for example, pages 
from old magazines, newspapers, etc. Draw an outline of an 
animal or object on a large piece of paper. The outline should be 
large (and spacious). Put glue on it and ask the children to tear 
pieces of paper given to them and paste the pieces of paper within 
the outline. The outline could be that of a tree, a hut, a ball, a 
cat, etc.
Variation
Ask the children to make a collage by cutting pictures from old 
magazines, newspapers, etc.
4 – 5 years 
Give the children simple shapes like squares or a triangle drawn 
on paper and get them to cut it neatly along the outline given. 
(Make sure the scissors are not very sharp or pointed).
Variation
Prepare name sheet for each child and ask them to do tearing 
-pasting activity using coloured paper/newspaper and paste on 
the initial letter of their name, they can also write their name 
using tearing-pasting activity.
5 – 6 years
Give the children pages of old newspapers, magazines, etc., and 
encourage them to make their own collage, by pasting the pieces 
in any design evolved by them. They could be given old cloth 
pieces also and other waste material, leaves, petals, etc., which 
they could use for pasting in the collage work.
Variation
Let the children decorate a shoe box or gift box by tearing pasting 
activity.
Other competencies 
addressed
Creativity and imagination, hand movement, precision and 
control, hand-eye coordination, differentiates shapes, colours; 
identifies and extends patterns.
What can be observed?
The child cuts, tears or pastes the desired shape and learns to do 
the same with precision, understands oral instructions, converses 
fluently, innovates, works imaginatively.


UNMUKH — Trainers’ Handbook for Balvatika
110
Activity 3
Drawing/Colouring/Painting
Material required
Crayons, water/natural colours paper, thread
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
Provide the children with crayons and an old newspaper or any 
other paper. Let them use the crayons freely to draw on the paper. 
Variation
Provide the children with some wet mud and give them a short 
stick or stem. They can scribble on the mud.
4 – 5 years 
Draw a given shape with smaller areas marked out and ask the 
children to colour the enclosed areas with different colours.
Variation
•	 Let the children use variety of colouring material including 
natural colours like haldi/flower colour, etc. 
•	 Let them draw rainy season scenery, draw and colour rainbow.
5 – 6 years
•	 Children can do spray-painting by dipping old tooth brushes 
in paint and using the bristles of the brush to spray the paint.
•	 Give the children specific themes and let them draw sceneries 
according to their own interpretation.
Variation
•	 Different types of painting can help strengthen a child’s hand-
eye coordination and manual dexterity.
•	 Finger painting gives children an opportunity to use their 
fingers, hands and to get messy. Painting with a brush helps 
them learn to hold a brush and gain greater control using it as 
a tool. 
•	 Put a colour-dipped thread between the paper with the tail of 
the thread hanging out. Put another sheet on top and let one 
child press hard while another pulls the thread out by the tail. 
A coloured design will emerge. Let children punch holes on the 
sheet and put the work in their file folder and create their own 
portfolio. 
Other competencies 
addressed
Eye-hand coordination, control, creativity and imagination, 
communicates and appreciates a variety of responses while 
creating art forms.
What can be observed?
The child draws, colours and paints with creativity and shows their 
imagination. The child actively participates with concentration. 
Explores and plays with a variety of materials and tools.


Annamaya Kosha and Pranamaya Kosha
111
Activity 4
Let us Make Patterns 
Material required
Pebbles, coloured beads, grains, shells, leaves, matchsticks
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
Provide blocks of different colours by mixing them up. Pattern 
activity can be done with a set of blocks.  Build the beginning of 
the pattern and ask children to extend it. Challenge them to tell 
you what the rule is (one red block, two yellow blocks, repeat). 
This will also take into account sorting and then lead to pattern 
making.
Variation
Organise scribbling activities and slowly move them to making 
patterns on sheet of paper with fingers dipped in colour.
4 – 5 years 
Draw a pattern on the board, keep it at eye-level and let the 
children select the things from beads, matchsticks, pebbles, etc., 
and copy the pattern with the selected item. This is how they will 
also learn to copy things from the board when they move to upper 
grades.
Variation
Make worksheets where children get the chance to practice 
patterns by drawing dots to join and draw.
5 – 6 years
Let the children sort pebbles, coloured beads, grains, shells, 
leaves, matchsticks, etc., according to colour, shape or size 
and make groups by pasting same things together like all 
pebbles in one circle, leaves in another circle, etc. Let them 
count how many pebbles? how many leaves? which is more? 
etc.
Variation
Mix alphabet cards and number cards together. Let the children 
sort them and place them in separate bowls. Let them make a 
pattern with them example one alphabet, one number, then one 
alphabet and so on.
Other competencies 
addressed
Concept 
of 
colour, 
shape, 
environmental 
awareness, 
classification, differentiates shapes, visual memory, sorts 
objects, comprehends.
What can be observed?
Shows problem-solving skills by sorting a set of blocks and 
other material and reflects memory power. The child uses 
observation skills and completes the pattern making activity, 
innovates and works imaginatively, explores and plays with 
variety of material.

Activity 5
Printing for Fun
Material required
Paper, colour and printing material
Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
•	 Block printing can be done. 
•	 Leaves dipped in colours can be used. Foot and hand printing 
can be done.
•	 Let the children put a slice of onion or a piece of ladyfinger into 
colour and print designs on paper with it.
•	 Make a shape or design. Cut the potato into halves. Let them 
dip it in colour and print it on paper.
Variation
Children can be asked to create drawings with finger dipped 
in colour, crayons, paint brushes, thumb printing, vegetable 
printing, etc.
4 – 5 years 
Collect a variety of materials that can be used to dip in paint 
and stamp on a paper to create its imprint, e.g., objects like a 
used comb, blocks wrapped with twine or rubber bands, old 
wooden toys and so on. Give used papers or newspapers to 
each child and they can then dip these objects in paint and 
stamp them on the paper to make imprints to enhance eye-
hand coordination.
Variation
Children can do thumb printing and hand printing and use their 
creativity to visualise and make drawings of anything like snail, 
frog, peacock, etc.
5 – 6 years
Children can make prints with different objects like vegetables, 
blocks, etc., on the paper. They can make a wrapping paper or 
paper bag out of it. 
Variation
Children can also do thumb or hand printing to create an image 
like cat, mouse, fish, etc.
Other competencies 
addressed
Eye-hand coordination, expressing emotions, spatial sense and 
creativity, understands and responds positively, shows care and 
joy, sorts, explores and plays with variety of material.
What can be observed?
The child uses various materials and depicts their creativity by 
printing their designs/patterns, memory, observation, wonder, 
communication.

Age group
How to conduct the activity?
3 – 4 years 
Let the children fold their handkerchiefs/paper using one or two

Variation
Let them fold their handkerchiefs using one or two folds in

4 – 5 years 
Children can be taught to make different objects with paper, e.g.,
